S-0. 1658. — A UccncG No. P/S/16.33308 dated the 26lh June, 1069 for the

pendix 32 of the Import Trade Control Red Book Vol. I for April, 1969 — March,

gan.i, Kanpur.

clusion that the licence In question will not serve the purpose for which it was 
'Issued.

is satisfied that the licence In question sho.uld be cancelled or otherwise rendered 
Inedectlve. Therefore, the undersigned, in exercise of powers vested In him 
'under clause 9 sub-dause (cc) of the Imports (Control) Order, 195,5 hereby can- 
cell“ the licence No. P/S/1 633308 dated the 26th June, 1969 for Rs. 2.00,000 
■'issued in favour of M/s. Progressive Industries, Kanpur.

S.O, 16C0. — A licence No- P/S/1651389 dated Jst September, 1969, for the value 
..of Rs. 8360 for the import of German Silver Scrap was issued to M/s- Indian 
Sanitary Ware, Chauraha Gali, Moradabad. 

Thereafter a Show Cause Notice No. Rnf. I (290) /1909/Kan dated the 3rd 
November, 1970 was issued asking them to show cause within 15 (Fifteen) days of 
the receipt of notice as to why the said licence in their favour should not be can- 
celled, on the ground that they had no factory In exi.stcnce and the licence was, 
therefore, not to serve the purpose for which it was issued. 

M/s. Indian Sanitary Ware. Moradabad replied to the .said show cause and 
. stated that the,y were not Interested in the said licence due to Its' small value. 

The undersigned has carefully considered the case and has come to the con- 
clusion that the licence in question will not serve the purpose for which it was 
Issued. 

Having regard to whet has been stated in the preceding para the undersigned Is 
satisfied that the licence in question should be cancelled or otherwise rendered 
iheffective. Therefore, the undersigned, In exercise of powers vested in him under 
clause 9 Sub-clause (cc) of the Imports (Control) Order, 1955 hereby cancells 
the licence No. P/S/ 1651389 dated the lat September, 1969, for Rs, 8,850 issued 
in favour of M/s, Indian Sanitary Ware, Chaurahagali, Moradabad.

Bangalore-18, were granted import licence No. P/S/ 1636028/C/XX/34/X/29-30. 
dated 22nd March, 1970, for Rs. 5,000 lor Import of Aromatic Chemicals and N. E. 
Oils. They have now applied for duplicate copy of Customs purpose copy a.s well, 
as Exchange Control purpose copy of the above licence on the ground that the 
original of the above Customs purpose and Exchange Control purpose copy of the- 
licence have been lost. It Is further .stated that the above licence is not at nil

purpose copy of the licence new required is for Rs. 5,000,

purpose copies of the above licence have been lost and direct that a duplicate copy

S,0. IGftfi. — Whereas hy the notification of the Government of India In the 
late Min stry of Health No F.16-14/ 59-MI, dated the 30th March. 1960 the Central 
Government has directed that the Medical qualification, “Doctor of Medicine” 
(Baylor University, School of Medicine, Taxas, U S.A,) shall be recognised medical 
qualiflcaiion for the purposes of the Indian Medical Council Act 1956 (102 of 1956); 

And whereas Dr. R, G. Burrows who possesses the said qualification Is for the 
time beiag attached to the Makunda Christian Medical-Agricultural Joint Seva 
Manrial, Post Bazaricherra, Cachar Distt. Assam for the purpose of charitable 
work; 

Now therefore, in pursuance of clause (c) of the proviso to sub-section (1) of 
section 14 of the .said Act, the Central Government hereby specifies — 

(1) a period upto and inclusive of 31st December, 1971 or 
(ij) the period during which Dr. R. G. Burrows is attached to the said 
Makunda Christian Medical-Agricultural Joint Seva Mandal, Po.st 
Bazaricherra, Cachar Distt. Assam whichever is shorter, as the period 
to which the medical practice by the aforesaid doctor shall be limited.

MINISTRY OF TOURISIM AND CIVIL AVIATION 

New Delhi, the 12th April 1971 

S.O. 1667. — Whereas on the 26th March, 1971, a Jainair Co. Private Ltd., Dakota, 
aircraft VT-ATT while on a passenger flight from Gauhati to Calcutta via Bagdogra 
crashed about 30 miles East-North East of Hashimara, resulting in the death of 11. 
passengers and 4 crew; 

And whereas it appears to the Central Government that It is expedient to hold'- 
formal investigation in the circumstances of the said accident; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by rule 73 of the Aircraft- 
Buies, 1937, the Central Government hereby directs that a formal investigation of 
the said accident be held; 

The Central Government is further pleased to appoint Shri S. K. Dutta, retired 
Judge of the Calcutta High Court, to hold the said ■■nve.sHgaiiQn. 

The Central Government Is also pleased to appoint; — 

(1) Group Capt. J. R. Bhasin, Director of Flight Safety, Air Headq,uarterg, 

New Delhi, and 

(2) Capt. B. S. Sareen, Retired Regional Director, Indian Airlines, New Delhi,, 
to act as assessors to the .said investigation.

S. N. KAUL, Dy. Secy-

CENTRAL EXCISE COLLECTORATE 

Manufactured Products 
New Delhi, the 5th January 1971 

Subject.— Delegation of Collectors powers under Rule 191-A and 191-B of Central 
Excise Rules, 1944: 

S.O. 1673- — In exercise of the powers conferred upon me by Rule 5 of the Central 
Exci.ie Rules, 1944, I hereby delegate to the Deputy Collector of Central Excise 
(Technical), Hdqrs. Office, Baroda, the powers for approval of manufacturing 
formula under Rule 191-A and 191-B of Central Excise Rules, 1944 In respect of

MINISTRY OF FOOD, AGRICULTURE, COMMUNITY DEVELOPMENT AND 

CO-OPERATION 

(Department of AKrlouItare) 

New Delhi, the 27th March 1971 

S.O. 1681. — Whereas a draft of the Cotton Grading and Marking Rules, 1070 
was published, as required by section 3 of the Agricultural Produce (Grading and 
Marking) Act, 1937 (1 of 1937), at pages 3151 to 3154 of the Gazelle of India, 
Part II, Section 3, Sub-section (ii), dated the 26th July, 1970, with the notification 
of the Government of India In the Ministry of Food. Agriculture, Community 
Development and Cooperation, (Department of Agriculture), No. S.O. 2459. dated 
the 10th July, 1970, Inviting objections and suggestions from all persons likely to 
be affected thereby, till the lOih August, 1970. 

And whereas the sold Gazette was made available to the public on the 26th 
July, 1970; 

And whereas no objections or suggestions have been received from the public 
on the said draft; 

Now, therefore. In exercise of the powers conferred by section 3 of the said 
Act and In supersession of the Cotton Grading Marking Rules, 1962, the Central 
Government hereby makes the following rules, namely: — 

RULES 

1. Short title and application.— (1) These rules may be called the Cotton Grad- 
ing and Marking Rules, 1971. 

(2) They shall apply to the varieties of cotton which are grown in India and 
Which are specified in Schedule I. 

2. Definition. — In these rules, 

(1) "Schedule” means a Schedule annexed to these rules, 

(2) "False or fraudulent packing" means packing cf a bale of cotton; 

(a) in such a manner as to contain in dlfCerent parts of the bale, cotton of 

different growths or varieties, or cotton of materially different staples, 
or cotton of different crops; or 

(b) in such a manner as to contain in any part of the bale any byeproducta 

such as waste, fly, or other byeproaucts of a cotton mill available 
after raw cotton is passed through the blow room; or 




Sec. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: APRIL 24. I97I/VAISAK.HA 4, 1893 2061 


(c) In such a manner as to contain In any part of the bale any concealed 

substance other than raw cotton; or 

(d) In such a manner as to contain interiorly cotton decidedly inferior to 

that upon the exterior and not readily detectable on the customary 
examination. 

.3. Grade Desiffnatloii and quality.— (1) The grade designations of the varieties 
of cotton spocilied in Schedule I shall be as specified In column 1 of Schedule II 
and the special characteristics and the general characteristics indicated by the 
grade designations shall be specified in column 2 and 3 respectively of Schedule II. 

(2) The grade de.signations referred to in sub-rule (1) shall be applied only to 
cotton in lull pressed bales. 

4. Grade designation marks. — (1) The grade designation mark shall consist of 
a label specifying the grade designation and bearing a design (consisting of an 
outline map of India with the word AGMARK and the figure of the rising sun with 
the words “Produce of India”) resembling, that set out In Schedule HI. 

(2) The design and grade designation shall be of the lohowing colour:— 


Grade designation Colour of designation and lettering of lebel 


Agmark Certified pedigreed Red 

Agmark Certified Black 


5. Method of Marking. — (1) The grade designation mark shall be placed agaln.st 
one or the other of the flat sides of bale, shall be fixed to the hessian cloth wrapper 
and shall be held securely in position by at least 3 hoops. 

(2) The grade designation mark shall clearly show the date of pressing, variety 
of cotton and place of growth (Block/District/State). 

(3) The affixing of the grade designation mark on an end hessian or an un- 
lashed side of a bale shall not be deemed to fulfil the requirements of this rule. 

0. Method of packliig. — (l) The cotton shall be packed in bales in the manner 
customary in the trade. 

(2) There shall be no false or fraudulent packing of the bales. 

ScHEDtTLE I 

[i?ee rules 1(2) and 3,) 

List of varieties of cotton avproved for Acfmarking 

1. A, 51-9 (Narmada). 

2. Adonicum. 

3. AK-235. 

4. AK-277. 

6. Andrews (Extra long staple)'. 

6. Badnawar 1 (C.T.I. 4-27). 

7. Burl 147. 

8. Burl 0394. 

9. C. Indore-I. 

10. C. Indore-2. 

11. Cocanada8-2. 

12. DeviraJ (170-C02),

ministrv of petroleum and chemicals and mines and metals 

(DepartmeiQt of Petroleum) 

New Delhi, the 20th March 1971 

S O. 1682, — Whereas in pursuance of Resolution of the Government of India 
In the Ministry of Petroleum and Chemicals and Mines and Metals (Department of 
Petroleum) No. 28(11) /70-OR, dated the 22n(l August, 1970, a Commission has 
been set up to inquire Into and report on various matters as set out in the terms 
of reference specified in the said Resolution; 

And whereas the Central Government Is of opinion that all the provisions of 
the Commissions of Inquiry Act. 1932 (60 of 1962) should be made applicable to 
the said Commission; 

Now, therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by section 11 of the said 
Act, the Central Government hereby directs that all the provisions of the said 
Act shall apply to the said Commission. 

[No. F. 28 (11)/70-OR.J 
E. N. MANGAT RAI, Spl. Secy.

S.O. 1683. — ^Whereas it appears to the Central Government that it is necessary 
In the public interest that for the transport of petroleum from DS. BEP. To G.G.S. 
3 Line, in Gujarat State, Pipelines should be laid by the Oil and Natural Gas 
Commission, 

And whereas it appears that for the purpose of laying such Pipelines, It Is 
necessary to acquire the Right of User In the land described In the schedule 
annexed hereto; 

Now therefore, in exercise of the powers conferred by sub-section (1) of the 
section ’3 of the Petroleum Pipelines (Acquisition of Rl.ght of User in Land) Act, 
1962 (50 of 1962), the Central Government hereby declares its Intention to acquire 
the right of user therein; 

Provided that any person interested In the said land may, within 21 days from 
the date of this notification, object to the laying of the pipelines under the land

S.O, 1684.— In pursuance of sub-rule (2) of rule 9, clause (b) sub-rule (2) 
of rule 12 and sub-rule (1) of rule 24, read with rule 34, of the Central Civil 
Services (Classification, Clontrol and Appeal) Rules, 1965, the President hereby 
makes the following further amendments in the notification of the Government 
of India in the Cabinet Secretariat No. S.R.O. 633 dated the 28th February, 1957, 
namely: — 

In the Schedule to the said notification. In Part II — General Central Service, 
Class III, under the heading ‘‘Directorate of National Sample Survey", 
for the entry ‘Chief Director, National Sample Survey’ occurring in 
column 2 against item (i) In column 1. the entry “Deputy Director 
(Admlnistrailon)’’ shall be substituted.

MINISTKY OF LABOUR, EMPLOYMENT AND REHABILITATION 
(Department of Labour and Employment) 

New Delhi, the 12th April, 1971 

S.O. 1686. — In pursuance of section 17 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 (14 
of 1947), the Central Government hereby publishes the following award of the 
Central Government Industrial Tribunal, Dhanbad, in the industrial dLspute bet- 
ween, the employ^ers in relation to the Dhansar ColUery of Messrs Pure Dhansar 
Coal Company, Post Office Dhansar, District Dhanbad and their workmen, which 
was received by the Central Government on the 6th April, 1971. 

BEFORE THE CENTRAL GOVERNMENT INDUSTRIAL TRIBUNAL AT 

DHANBAD 

In the matter of a reference under section 10(1) (d) of the Industrial Disputes 
Act, 1947. 

Reference No. 68 of 1968 

Parties: 

Employers in relation to the Dhansar Colliery of Messrs Pure Dhansar Coal 
Company, Post Office Dhansar, District Dhansar. 

AND 

Their workmen. 

Present; 

Shri A. C. Sen, Presiding Officer. 



Sec. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: APRIL 24, 1971 /VAISAK:ha 4, 1893 2077 


Appearances; . 

B'or lh '2 r,ir:%iloyers Shii S. S. Mukherjee, Advocate, with Shrl B. Joshi, 
Advocate, 

For the Workmen — Shri Lalit Burman, General Secretary, Bihar Koyla 
Mazcioor Sabha, Dhanbad. 

State: Bihar. Industry; CoaL 

Dhanbad, dated the 3rd Avril. 1971 

AWARD 

The above Reference arises out of the Order of the Central Government under 
clause (d) of sub-section ( 1 ) of section 10 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, 
being order No. 1125i68-LRII, dated New Delhi, the 11th October, 1988 by whicli 
the following dispute was refei’red to this Tribunal: 

Schedule 

“(i) Whether the management of Dhansar Colliery of Messrs Pure Dhansar 
Coal Company, Post Office Dhansar, District Dhanbad was lustifled m 
dismissing S]Shri Jago Gope and Kis'hun Singh, Underground tram- 
mers, with effect from the 14th August, 19687 If not, to what relief 
are these workmen entitled? 

(ii) Whether the management of Dhansar Colliery of Messrs Pure Dhansar' 
Coal Company, Post Offlco Dhansar. District Dhanbad is iustified in 
not making payment of profit sharing bonus to the workmen for the- 
accounting year 1966-67? If not, to what relief are the workmen 
entitled?” 

1. I intend to deal with the two items separately. At first, I take up the ques- 
tion of dismissal, which is the subject matter of the first item, The concerned 
workmen at all relevant times were the employee under the management of 
Dhansar Colliery of Messrs Pure Dhansar Coal Company owned by partners. 
The workmen sa.y that the concerned workmen were the permanent employees 
of the .said colliery with a clean record of service over a long period. According 
to them the management deprived the workers of the colliery of legitimate benefits 
like quarterly bonus, profit sharing bonus, provident fund, leave wages, slcknes.s 
allowance etc. Their further case is that as the management refused to imple- 
ment the recommendations of the Centi;al Wage Board, which were to be imple- 
mented from the 1st January. 1967, there was a great discontent among the work- 
ers of the colliery, who organised a (branch of the Bihar Koyla Mazdoor Sabha in 
the month of July, 1967 to ventilate their grievances through their organisation. 

2. Shri Jago Gope and Kishun Singh, the workmen concerned were elected,, 
they say, as President and Secretary respectively of the Dhansar Colliery branch 
of the union and their lawful trade union activities were much disliked by the 
employers who were constantly trying to break the unity of the workers and to. 
disrupt the union. 

3. In paragraph 5 of the Workmen’s written statement it has been stated that 
on the face of organised movement of the workers and as a result of the inter- 
vention of the Labour Department the management had to agree to the impli- 
mentation of the Coal Wage Board recommendations only from the 15th August 
1967. Mr. Burman, appearing on behalf of the workmen, said at the hearing 
that “1967” was typing mistake, that it should be “1968.” An applicgtion for 
amendment, properly verified, .should have been filed in time. A.s that has not 
been done I shall proceed on the fooling that it is “1967” as given in the written 
statement and not “1968” as suggested by Mr. Burman. 

4. Tliey accuse the management of victimising the local organisers of the 

union for pulling pressure on the management to implement the recommenda- 
tions of the Central Wage Board for Coal Mines, which they ultimately agreed 
to do with cltsc;: from tlie 15'Ti August, 1967. The relevant portion from their 
v’l'itten s . dement is quoted below; “while the management agreed to implement 
the Wage Board recommendations only from 15th Au,gust 1967 decided to 
rictimi.se thr ioc;! ors'-jin-'s-T ■. of -^he Union from '4th .^'igu.st, 1967, -i.g. ju- t one 
lay before the date of Implementation of Wage Board recommendations with a 
clear int.entlon.s to 1 er.i.'o.rise tl-y other v.n-''cevs end to coerce the other 

leading organisers of the Union.” Mr. Burman at the time of hearing asked me- 
to read “IfGO” for “1967” on the ground of typing mistake, but for the reason 
already staled I shall proceed on the footing that it is “1967” as given In tho 
written statement and not “1968” as suggested Iby Mr. Burman, 



2078 the gazette of INDIA; APRIL 24, 1971 A'AISAKHA 4, 1893 [Part II— 


5. The workmen have challenged the enquiry proceeding in these words; 
"That the management did not hold any proper enquiry over the charge and 
did not grant any reasonable opportunity to defend the affected workmen and 
the management has dismissed the affected workmen on the basis of perverse 
findings, which is not legally maintainable, because the management has violated 
the principles of natural justice in dismissing the affected workmen with a clear 
intention to victimise the affected workmen”. 

0. The employers version is as follows. On 20th July 1968 and again on 22nd 
July 1908 Jago Gope and Kis'hun Singh, the workmen mentioned in item No. 1 
of the schedule of reference along with two others, who were all under ground 
trammers threatened to assault the attendance clerk while he was issuing oil to 
them. For the above misconduct individual charge sheet dated 26th July 1968 
were issued to the workmen concerned and two others who threatened to assault 
the attendance clerk. All the four workmen submitted a joint reply dated 28th 
July 1968 denying the charges, A departmental enquiry was held in the presence 
of the said workmen on 8th August 1968 when they Were given full chance 
and opportunity to cross examine the witnes.scs for the management and produce 
defence witnesses, 

7, It is the definite case of the employers that the said four workmen includ- 
ing the two concerned in the present case participated in the enquiry by cross 
examining wilncsscs and producing one witness in defence. They, however, say 
that the workmen or the defence witness did not put their left thumb Impression 
on the minute.’ of the enquiry proceeding. Their further case is that in the above 
departmental enquiry the mi.sconduct mentioned in the charge sheet, dated 26th 
July, F68 was sad fnctorily established against Jago Gope and Kishun Singh 
the workmen concerned, who were, therefore, dismissed by a letter dated 14th 
Augu.st, 1068. 

8. First cf all let me consider whether the departmental enquiry was held. 
In their wrltlen statement the workmen have said tnat the n-iunagcment did not 
hold any proper enquiry over the char.ge.s and did not grant any rea.sonablc op- 
portunity to workmen concerned to defend themselves. It is not their case in 
the written statement that enquiry wa.s not held at all. But at the hearing Mr. 
Eurman on their behalf took up the position that there was no enquiry. Materials 
on the record clearly establi.sh that an enquii-y was held. 

9, A joint notice dated 26th July, 1968 was served on the two workmen con- 
cerned and two others to show cause why^disciplinary action would not be taken 
agamst them (Ext.M — 1). All the four workmen sent a joint reply, dated 28th 
July, 1988, denying the allegations (Ext. M — 2). All the four workmen were in- 
formed by the Manager by a letter dated the .31st July, 1968 that an enquiry 
into the chargesheet would be held on the 0th August 1968 at 10 A.M. in the 
Manager’s ofhee. That this letter was received by the workmen concerned is 
proved by their documents Exts. W6 and W7, both dated 7th August 1.968. W7 is 
a letter written by Jago Gope to the Manager which runs as follov/s: “With 

reference to your letter No dated the 31st July, 1968 informing me that the 

enquiry into the charge-sheet. .. .will be held at 10 A.M. on the 6th instant, I 
have to inform you that although I presented myself for the enquiry no such 
enquiry was held”. Ext, W0 is also to the same effect. Ext. W4 is a letter dated 
6th August, 1968, written to Kishun Singh, one of the workers concerned, by 
the Manager to say that as 'he did not appear for enquiry on the plea that he 
would tender apologies and as no apology was subsequently submitted enquiry 
vmuld be held at 10-30 A.M. on the 8th August, 1968 in the Manager’s office. 
FiXt. W5 is a similar letter addressed to Jago Gope. These two letters must have 
been received by the workmen concerned because they have been filed by them. 
Tlicre was no denial to the allegations made in Exts, W4 and W.t. Though 
kixts W6 and W7 wore written by the concerned workmen on the 7th August, 
1908, there was no mention of the letter of the manager dated 6th August, 1968. 
Exts. W6 and W7 were evidently written with the idea of utilising them in 
future to show that no enquiry was held, 

10. I have no doubt in my mind that an enquiry was held on the 8th Augusf, 
1968 and that the concerned workmen took part in that enquiry. MWl at first 
s-'rys that company issued notice for holding enquiry on two days and that on 
both the days he and others presented themselves for the enquiry. Then he 
■says that no enquiry was held. He also says that He and others wrote a letter 
to the management informing that no enquiry had been held. He evidently 
refers to Exts W6 and W7. One would have expected similar letters from the 
workmen concerned if no enquiry had been held on the 8th August 1968. On the 
other hand, MWl, Malvern In his deposition has clearly stated that he held the 



Sec. 3(ji)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: APRIL 24, I971/VAISAKHA 4, 1893 2079 


enquiry on the 8th August in the presence oi the concerned workmen and two 
others and that they were given lull opportunity to cross-examine all the 
management witnesses. The enquiry proceeding, Ext. MIO has also been filed. 
I have no reason to disbelieve Malvern nor have I any reason to think that 
Ext. MIO was fabricated subsequently. Having regard to the admitted docu- 
nients in relation to the charge sheet and the enquiry and having regard to the 
relations between the parties it is highly improbable that the concerned work- 
nien were dismissed without any enquiry whatsoever and without giving the 
worker opportunity to cross-examine the witness produced Tjy the management. 
On the evidence on record I am satisfied that an enquiry into the allegations 
m ade in the charge-sheet was held on 8th August 1968, that the concerned work- 
men and two others served with the charge sheet participated in the said 
enquiry and that they had full opportunity to cross-examme the management 
witnessc.s and to produce their own witness. 

11. The next point for consideration is whether the finding of the enquiry 
oificer that the concerned workmen are guilty of the acts of misconduct men- 
tioned in the charge sheet is supported by the materials on lecord. One witness 
Dasrath Mahto, was produced by the workmen charged with misconduct at the 
departmental enquiiy. He deposed as follow.s: “I hoard some alteration between 
Jago Gopc and Kishun Singh and Hanree Babu about charge-sheet....! did not 
hear anybody abusing when 1 was at the attendance room.” He had earlier 
said that trammers had gene down the mine i:arlier than the miners. The con- 
cerned workmen are trammers and the witness is a miner. The enquiry officer 
ha.s given the following reason for disbelieving him: ‘Tie slated that he did not 
hear any-body abusing Sri Lachman Munda, Ibut he confirms that some alter- 
cation wms going on between Jago Gope, Kishun Singh and Lachman Munda. 
Elutcmcnt of Dashrath Mahto is contradictory and hence cannot be relied.” In 
my opinion lie was not justified in rejecting his evidence on the ground stated 
b;r him. In the first instance, the statement of Dashrath Mahto cannot be said 
to be contradictory. If there is .some altercation between two persojis it docs 
not necessarily follow that one will abuse another. The statement ol this wit- 
ness amounts to this; there was altercation between Kishun Singh and Jago 
Gopc on the one hand and Lachman Munda on the other, but tiic workmen con- 
corr.ed did not abuse Lachman Mundu, the attendance clerk. It should be remem- 
bered that Dashrath Mahto was not at all cross-examined on behalf of the man- 
agement. The enquiry officer was not ,iii.stificd in rejecting the evidonoe of 
Dashrath Mahto. 

12, The attendance clerk. Lachman Munda .said as follows in his examination 
in chief regardin.g the incident of the 22nd July, 1968: ‘‘They again abused me 
on 22nd July 1968 in Til shift while I was is.suing oil to them because I reported 
the incident of 20th July 1968 to the Manager.” He did not say that the work- 
men concerned threatened to assault him. Yet the enquiry officer says that 
“Sihri Lachman Munda also complained of having been threatened by Shri Jago 
Gope and Kl.shun Singh again on "2nd July 1968 while issuing oil to them in 
in .shift.” Jagcli^b Prasad improved ur/on the statement of the attendance clerk 
by .saying that Kishun Singh and Jago Gope abused the attendance clerk and 
threatened to break his hand.s and legs. He wa.s deposing regarding the inci- 
dent ol the 22nd July, 1968. In his cros.s-examination he confessed that he did 
not know on what matter Kishun Sin.gh wa.s abusing the attendance clerk. 

18. A.S to the incident of 20th July, 1908, the evidence of Baijnath Pra.sad 
practically proves nothing. In his examination in chief he said, “When I came 
to attendance room Jago Gope and Kishun Singh were abusing and threatening 
Lachman Munda. I a.sked these p-ersons as to wlw they arc abusing attendance 
clerk.” The work ‘threatening' is rather vague. He did not .say that they were 
threatening to assault, which was in the charge sheet. Immediately thereafter 
he said that he asked the person.s as to why they were aJbusing the attendance 
clerk. This shows that by the expression ‘abusing and threatening, he really 
meant ‘abusing.’ But in the charge sheet the misconduct mentioned In ‘threat to 
a!i.sault.’ So. the enquiry officer is not Justified In saying that Baijnath Prasad 
corroborated the te.stimony of the attendance clerk an^ of M. P, Paswan. It Is 
clear that the enquiry officer was labouring under a misconception that the 
words “threatened the attendance clerk” and the words “threatened to assault 
the clerk” mean the same thing. It is needless to point out that the word ‘threat’ 
is rather vague, while the words ‘threat to assault’ mean something definite, 

14, The nature of the finding is such that it at once strikes one as biasOC 
For the reasons stated above and also on the grgund of bias _1 am constralnedi 
to hold that the finding of the enquiry officer is perverse, and is liable to be set 
aside. 



208 o IHE gazette of INDIA: APRIL 24, 197I/VAISAKHA 4, 1393 [Part II~ 


15. El my opinion the order of dismissal too has not been passed in accord- 
ance v/iin me slandin^ orders Clause (iv) of Standing Order No. 17 reads as 
foliovvs; "In awarding pcniahni^nt under this Standing order, the authority 
arcai'ding punisinnent shan taiie into account the gravity of the misconduct, tJie 
previous record, if any, or uie workman and any other extenuating or aggravat- 
ing circuirisUmccs that may exist....” Exts. WB and W9 are the letters of dis- 
missal addressed to the concerned workmen. The.y are in identical langaage 
These letters of dismissal were issued over the signature of D. P. Lodha, Partner, 
for Pure Dhansar Coal Co. Tnere is nothing in any of these two letters of dis- 
ndssal which shows that the authority awarding punishment, namely the part- 
ners of Pure Dnansar Coal Co., took into account lire gravity of the misconduct, 
the previous record, if any, of the workmen concerned and other extenuating 
or aggravating circumstances that might exist. The relevant portion of Ext. W8, 
letter of dismissal issued to Kishun Sin^h, is quoted below: "With reference to 

chargo sheet No and reply thereto, a departmental enquiry was held Un 

8ith August, 1958 in your presence when you were given full chance and oppor- 
tunity to cross-examine the witnesses and defend yourself. In the above depart- 
meniai enquiry the misconducts mentioned in the above charge sheet have been 
E.iitisfactorily established. You are, therefore, dismissed from service with im- 
mediate ellect.” 

Ifi. Shiva Kumar Lodha. MW2 admitted in his cross-examination that he did 
noi know whether the entire matter was considered by the other partners Ibefore 
passing order of dismissal against Kishun Singh and Jago Gope. The docu- 
nienis filed by die management e.g. Ext. M14, show that the colliery is owned 
by four partners, namely, (1) Sm. Nanhi Bai Lodha, (2) Shiva Kumar Lodha, 
(:3) Debi Prasad Lodha, and (4) Baijnath Iiodha. Both the letters of dismissal 
were signed by D. P. Lodha as partner for Pure Dhansar Coal Co. D. P. Lodha 
obviously stands for Delhi Prasad Lodha. Evidence on record shows that the 
question of dismissal was not considered by all the partners in the light of 
clause (iv) cf Standing Older No. 17. The order of cii.smiFSai is liable to be set 
aside on this ground also, namely, that It was passed m violation of Standing 
Order No. 17 Uv,'. 

17, Such consideration by all the partners was all the more necessary in view 
of the fact tl.at for the particular misconduct manUuned m the charge .sheet, 
being a misconduct under Standing Order No. 17(1) (e), dismissal is not the only 
punnlimeni to be awarded. Clause (i) of Standing Order No. 17 prtivides that 
a workman imiy be suspended or fined or his Increment may be stopped or he 
may be demoled or dismissed without notice, if he is found tc be guilty of mis- 
conduct. The partners should have considered tho dcsirability_ of passing a 
lesser punishment than dismissal. The tone of the letter.s of dismissal clearly 
indicate that the partners were too eager to grasp the opportunity of dismissing 
the concevned workmen. Materials on record show that Ihe relation between 
the management and the workmen was' far from happy. The management ought 
to have taken extra precautio In. dismming the concerned workmen. 


18. The order of dismissal appears to be bad on another ground. Clause (li) 
of Standing Order No- 17 requires that the approval of the owner, agent or the 
Chief Milling Engineer of the employer or a person holding similar position 
shall be obtained before Imposing the punishment of dismissal. In this case both 
the letters of dismissal were Issued by D. P. Lodha. He Is only one of the Joint 
owner.s. There is nothing on record to show that the dismissal of the concerned 
workmen was anproved by the other partners' too, Then again, from the depo- 
sition of Shiva Kumar Lodha it transpires that it is he who looks after the man- 
agement of the colliery as a partner of the concern, The letter of dismissal, in 
any event, should have been issued by him as the managing partner and not by 
D. P. Lodha. Then it could perhaps have been said that the dismissal had the 
mibroval of a person holding a nosition similar to that of an agent or the Chief 
Min.ing Engineer. However In this case the letters of dismissal were Is'^ued by 
D. P. Lodha on behalf of the owner, s, but these two letters do not show that 
dlsmi.ssal had the approval of pll the partners. Tlie order of dismissal is liable 
to toe set aside on that ground also. 

19. The next question for consideration is whether the* management of Dhan- 
sar Colliery Is iu.stlfied in not making paymont of profit sharing bonus to thelr 
workmen for the accounting period 1966-1967; if not. to what relief they are 
entitled. Shiva Kumar Lodha, MW2 haa admitted in his cross-examination that 
the workmen are entitled to get the profit sharing bonus for the year 1966-87 
as per law. But his case is that tho profit sharing bonus for the accounting year 
1966-67 Is to be paid by Indra Kumar Agarwalla, if not already pedd- 



Sec. 3(ii)] TliE GAZEITE OF INDIA; APRIL 24, 1971/VALsAKHA 4. 1893 208l 


20. Oil the quqstion of profit sparing' bonus the employers case is follows. 
FrOiii 1103 lo_ June, 10G7 the Dhansar Colliery was uncL’r the raanagoment and 
coriLioi of Sri indra- Kumar Aear'Walla as managing agent. The jpresent man- 
agement took over charge of the colliery from the 3rd July, 196'f7 Before the 
Jrd July, 196V, the present management had no employer and employee relation- 
ship with the workmen of the colliery. 

21. The further case of the employers is that under the terms of the agree- 
ment between Indra Kumar Agarwalla and the present management, it waa 
Agarwalia who was responsible for the payment of all dues includi'ng p-rplit shar- 
ing bonus to the workmen. In their written statement they have stated that 
Indra Kumar Agarwalla is a necessary party for the proper and effective adjudi- 
cation of the dispute regarding the payment of profit sharing bonus for the 
accounting year 1968-67,

Industrial Disputes Act, il/iV rn.y predecessor-in-office pa;;3";d an orchsr on the 3rd 
.April, 1970 iinpleading Indra Kumar AgarwaUa as a party to the proceeding.

23. The memorandum ol afit'cement dated the 2nd September, 1963 between 
Indra .Kiiiaar Agarwalla and the Lodhas, who ebnsthute the present manage- 
ment lias been filed by the Lodhas, and it has Ibecn marked as Ext. M13. The 
Lodha’s are tne hciders of the leasehold interest in the c;jllicry. By an agree- 
ment dated the Vtii .June, 1955 the Lodhas appointed Ba,-jant,a' Kumar Agarwalla 
a:; their Agent to work the colliery. The said agreement having been termi- 
nated by mutual consent before the expii-y of the term the Lodhas entered into 
a 'ire.sh agreement on the 2nd September. 1&63 v/ith Indra Kumar Agarwalla for 
tne management and working of the colliery (Ext. M13). Clause 6 of M13 says 
that during the sub.sistence of the agreement, Indra Kumar Agarwalla describ- 
ed as the Agent shall have full charge and control of the mines, quarries, in- 
clines etc. of the colliery. Clause 13 provides that Indra Kumar Agarwalla as 
Agent shall employ such staff and employees and labour as are required for the 
efficient working of the mine, and thart the Agent shall be fully responsible for 
Iba salary and remuneration and for p-ayment of their provident fund, bonus and 
other dues, under clause 15 of the Ext. M13 the Lodhas are entitled to a share 
■of profit at the rate of 8 per cent of the net sale proceeds of the coal de.spatched 
subject to a minimum of Rs, 18000 per year, Clause 25 gives the Agent option 
to terminate the agency by giving six months’ notice but precludes the Lodhas 
from terminating the agency before the Lst day of September, 1980. 

24. Indra Kumar Agarwalla In exercise of the option given to him by clause 

25 gave notice on the 17th December, 1966 asking the Lndhas to take posses- 
sion of the colliery on and from the 1st July, 1967, The contract of agency thus 

c;uT.e to an end on the 30th June, 1967. It is, therefore, clear that io the account- 
ing year 1966-67 Indra Kumar Agarwalla was In absolute control of the colliery. 

He was, therefore, the employer within the meaning of clau.se (14) of sec. 2 of 

the Payment of Bonus Act, 1965. Again under clau.se 13 of the agency afirce- 
ment Indra Kumar Agarwalla was to employ such staff and labour as were: re- 
quired for the efficient management of the colliery and he was responsible for 
paying them their salaries, wages, bonus etc. 

25. From the judgment delivered on the 7th August, 1968 (Ext. M8) in T.R, 
Case No, 389 of 1968IC.W.W. Case No. 71 of 1967 it appears that Indra Kumar 
Agarwalla was found guilty of an offence under section 73 of the Mines Act as 
the owner and was sentenced to pay a fine of Rs. 125. Three out of the four 
Lodhas were also tried along with Indra Kumar Agarwalla but thev were acquit- 
ted Another judgment delivered on the 7th August, 1968 (Ext. M8) shows that 
though Indra Kumar Agarwalla was tried along with three of the Lodhas for 
the contravention of sec. 73 of the Mines Act, he alone was found guilty end 
convicted as owner and the Lodhas were acquitted. The three Lodhas were 
tried for the contravenffon of section 9(a) and (b) of the Coal Mines Provident 
Fund and Bonus Scheme Act, 1948, but they were acquitted on the ground that 
t’^ey did not constitute the owner within the meaning of that Act. The judg- 
ment in that case was delivered on the 17th May, 1969 (Ext. M9). 

26. In paragraph 3 of the letter dated the I7th October, 1966 (Ext. Ml 4;) 
Indra Kumar Agarwalla requested the Lodhas to take charge of the colliery and 
the ccll'ei’v business from him on fhe 1st July, 1987, subject to and in accordance 
with the relevant clau.sc.s of the A.greement, dated the 2nd September, 1963 (Ext. 



2082 GAZETTE OF INDIA: ATRIL 24, 1971/VAlSAKHA 4, J893 [Part U — 


MIO). It has been stated above that under the said Agreement India Kumar 
Agarwalla was responsible and liable for the salary and remuneration and lor 
payment ol provident fund, bonus ’and other dues. He had also lull povv^er to 
employ and discharge the employees and staff including the Manager, 

27. The Lodhas wrote a letter to India Kumar Agarwalla on the 18th Feb- 
ruary, 19G7 (Ext. M15). Item (d) of the said letter runs as follows; “Arrange 
to terminate the services ol all employees and workmen appointed bv you at 
the coiliery giving them timely notice of termination of their services including 
the Mines Manager. You should, therefore, also arrange to pay them all their 
due upto the 30th June, lt‘67, Including salary, dearness allowance, provident

on the Gth Juno, 1987, clause (b) of which reads as follows: “As far item (d), 
under the terms of the above agreement, it is not obligatory on my part to 
terminate the services of the employees, workmen and the Manager in .^crvice 
at the colliery. I am, however, arranging to pay all their dues on account of 
their salary, dearness allowances, etc. upto and including the 30th June, 1967. 
From the 1st July, 1967, payment of salar.v etc. of all the colliery .staff and Mines 
Manager and workmen will be yours,” The word "etc.” after “dearness allow- 
ance” makes it clear that Indra Kumar Affarwalla undertook to pay profit sharing 
bonus as well. 

28. Ap'art from the undertaking given by Indra Kumar Agarwalla in his let- 
ter dated the 6th June, 1067. under the law too he alone is bound to pay the 
profits sharing bonus. Section 8 of the Payment of Bonus Act, 1965 provides 
that every employee shall be entitled to be paid by his employer In a account- 
ing year, bonus, in accordance with the provisions of this Act, provided he has 
worked in the establishment for not less than thirty working days in that year. 
The word.s “in an accounting year” qualify the words “his employer.” Section 
8 really means that an employee is entitled to be paid bonus by his employer in 
an accounting year. Section 19 says that all amounts payable to an employee' 
by way of bonus shall be paid in cash by his employer when there is no dispute 
pending under section 22 within a period of eight months from the close of the 
accounting year, The employee must look to his employer In the accounting 
year for the payment of the bonu.s in respect of the accounting year. 
It i', therefore, clear that Indra Kumar Agarwalla wa,s respon,sible for the pay- 
m'^nt of the bonus due for the accounting year 1966-1967, because ho was the 
employer within the meaning of the Payment of Bonus Act, 1965. There is little 
sub.stance in the argument of Mr. Burman appearing on behplf of workmen that 
the Lodhas arc liable to pay the profit .sharing honu.s as under clause 15 of the 
Agreemont dated the 2nd September, 1963 they v/crc entitled to claim a share 
Of the '.'jroflt. Clause 1.5 really provides for con.sidevation for the right given to 
Inch'fi Kumar Agarwallo to remain in exclusive porsofsion and control cf the 
colliery during the subsistence of the agreement. Clause 15 merely lays down 
the method of calculating the amount to he paid to the Lodhas Kubject tn a 
guaranteed minimum of Rs. 18000 per annum. CMuse.15 has not the effect, of 
making the Lodhas partners of Indra Kumar Aparwalla. The Lodhas cannot 
be m::4e liable under chmse 15 of the Agreement. The Lodhas cannot be regard- 
ed a.s the successor-in-interest of India Kumar Agarwalla; therefore, even under 
the general law they cannot be made liable for the payment of tho profit .sharing 
bonus for the accounting year 1966-67. 

29. I accordingly award as follow.s: So far as item (i) of the Schedule is 
concerned the management of Dhnnsar Colllerv was pot iustifled in dismissing 
Shri Jngo Gnpe and Kishun Singh with' effect from 14th August, 1968; and the 
two concerned workmen, Kishun Singh and Jago Gone are entitled to be re- 
instated in their service with effect from the 14th August. 1968 with back wages 
from that date after deducting the amount, if any. received by them from the 
employers on account o-f wages or other benefit in the mean time. 

As to item (ii) of the Schedule, the management of Dhansar Colliery was not 
j-ustifled in not making payme'nt of profit sharing bonus to ihe work- 
men for the accounting year 1966-67; and the workmen are entitled to claim 
their profit scaring bonus for the said accounting year from Indra Kumer Agar- 
walla who managed the Colliery in the said accoim+ing vear and oof from the 
pre'-ent management who took over charge on and from the 1st July, 1967.

Specific Matters in Dispute 

"Whether the management o£ the Jamuria A/B Pits Colliery of M/s. Equit- 
able Coal Company Limited Po't Office Nandi, District Burdivan was 
justified in dismissing Shri Shyamdeo Singh Depot Guard from 16th 
July, 1970? If not, to what relief is the workman entitled." 

The Arbitration agreement dated 13th October, 1970 also provided that arbitra- 
tor shall mat.re award within a period of six months or within .such further time 
as Is extended by mutual agreement between the parties In wr.tirg, 

2- On 10th December, 1970 both the parties vmre reque.sled to submit their 
written .staloment To me by 31st December, 1970 with sirnultaneously/endorslng 
a copy to the opposite party. The employers and the Union both prayed lor 
furthei time to .submit Iheir written statements. This prayer was grunted and 
subsequently while the employers submitted their written statement on 21. st Janu- 
ary, 1971, the Union filed no ststement. The dispute came up for hea’lng at 
Asansol on 24th March, 1971. Shri H. R. Dae Giipia, Peri^onnel Officer and 
Shri R, S, Rathore, Administrative Assistant represented the employers tha

hearing and Ihe workman was represented by Shri S. K. Rudrai, General Secretary 

tJsTk organising Secretary, The hearing was concluded on 

45th March. 1971 at Asansol, 

3- Besides the written statement dated 21st January, 1971 which was tiled by 
the employers by post Shri H. R. Das Gupta, Personnel Officer filed the loUowlac 
documents during the hearing on 24th March, 1971:— 

(i) Ghaigesheet No. 64 of 12th November, 1969 along \^lth encjuiry proceed' 

Ings and report of the Enquiry Officer (17 sheets);

U Carcl); and 

(lii) Tripartite Settlement dated 22nd November, 1968 arrived at before the 

ALC(C), Asansol. (1 sheet). 

4. While presenting the case of the employers, Shri Das Gupta stated inter alia 
that Shri Shyamdeo Singh was appointed in the. Colliery In 1948- On 10th Novem- 
ber, 1969 the Vi^ovkman was on duty from 8 A.M. to 4 P.M, At about 3.30 P.M- 
on i.hat day, while the workman was on duty at the Di.pot of the Colliery, he 
neglected his duty inasmuch ns some outsiders were taking coal from the Depot 
without a'liy aulhorused slips. Shri Budhan Mahato, Empty Wagon Checking 
mazdoor who wa.s a.'so on duty observed that the workman was keeping silent and 
was not checking the unauthorised persons taking coal. Shri Mahato. therefore, 
forced the unauthorised persons to un,load the coal and he complained to another 
Guard, Shri Chutar Singh about the incident when the workman got annoyed and 
started abusing Shri Mohato. Thereafter, the workman approached Shri Mahato 
with a slick and all of a sudden .struck him with the same, There were other 
per.mns, who were wilne.ss to the incident and they separated Shri Mahato from 
the workmen. Shri Mahato was also seen weeping by some other workmen, The 
workman was chargeshoeted on ;i2th November, 1969 for abusing and assaulting 
Shri Budhan Mahato during the duty hours. Shri Uas Gupta slated that the 
chargesheet wa® given for misconduct under Clause 2‘:(5} cl the cerlllled Standing 
Orders iiipplicable to the establi.shment. The Labour Officer of the Collier.y. Shri 
A, K. Tarafdar was appointed as an Enquiry Officer, who after serving due notices 
held the enquiry on 16th May, 1970. During the enquir.y, 5 prosecution witnesses 
deposed evidences before the Enquiry Officer. These included 'Shri Budhan 
Mahato, who was abused and assaulted, one Khalasi, one Fitter helper, one Loading 
Clerk and the Medical Officer, who had treated Shri: Budhan Mahato for his injury. 
The delinquent produced only one witness viz. Shri Chutar Singh, Guard- Shri 
Das Gupta! maintained that the delinquent was given lull opportunity to cross 
examine the prosecution witnesses and the delinquent actually cross examined 
4 prosecution witnesses and declined to cross examine PW. 2. Shri Sabrati Mia, 
S. P, Khalasi. The Enquiry Officer submitted his report on 20th May, 1970 
hold.l.ng that the charge framed agMn.st the delinquent were proved beyond all 
reasonable doubts. Thereafter, the Manager of the Colliery on 22nd Maf.y, 1970 
recommended dismissal of the workman to the then Deputy Superintendent and 
also the Agent of the Ponlatl Group. On 1.3th July, 1970, the Agent of the CoUiery 
ordered clismis.sal of the workman and finally on 16th July, 1970. the dismlssap) 
order wa.s served on Shri Shyamdeo Singh- Summing up hi.s case, Shri Das Gupta 
stated that the workman was In the habit of abusing, quarreffing end actually 
fighting with his co-workmen and superiors as would be obvious from his service 
records. The employers' case was therefore, that the dismissal of the wmrkraan Is 
iustifieci, legal, bonafled and in accordance with the approved Standing Orders, 
Shri Das Gupta, therefore, prayed to give award accordingly. 

5. Shri S. K. Rudra. while presenting the case on behalf of the workman, 
stated in brief, that the chargesheet given to the workman was vague as it did not 
specify the time of the incident and did not elaborate the type of abuse hurled 
on Shri Mahato- Shri Rudra further maintained' that the findings of the Enquiry 
Officer were perverse and the charge ffs contained Ini the chargesheet was not at 
all proved. According to the Union, as the enquiry was not thorough and proper 
and os the charge was not proved, the dismissal of the workman was wrongful 
and illegal. Summing uu his arguments. Shri Rudra prayed thait in view of the 
aforesaid position the dismissal of Shri ^hyamcleo Singh was not justified and 
"hence the workman deserves reinstatement. 

6 The Union has built up its case mainly on three ground? — the vagueness of 
the charge-sheet, blwed and perverse enquiry proceedings and want of conclusive 
proof of the charge of abuse and assmilt. Let the factual position in regard to 
these points he now analysed- Shri Shyamdeo Singh was on di:ty from 8 A M. 
to 4 P.M. on inth November. 1969 near the Depot is a fact, wlilch is not disputed. 
The chargesheet No. 64 served on the workman on ■2th November, 1969 at the 



me. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: APRIL 24. 1971/VAlSAKHA 4, 1893 2085 


very beginning gives the date and time of incident as “lOth November, 1969 at 
4 PM” Theretore, the argument of Shri Rudra that the chargesheet is vague 
in regard to date and time of the incident, hence falls to ground. A to the 
content of the chargesheet the relevant entry is: 

“For abusing and assaulting Shri Budhan Mahato. Empty wagon checking 
mazdoor when he protested against robbing of coal by others from 
the depot during the duty hours. Thus you have v.olated Standing 
Order No. 27(5)”. 

Clause 27(5) of the certified Standing Orders enumerates the following acts 
as misconduct: — 

“27(5) Drunkenness, fighting, riotous or disorderly or indecent behaviour”. 

7 It is thus seen that while the Standing Orders makes mention in general of 
certain acts, the chagesheet particulars the miseonnuct viz “abuse’ and 
“assault”. The argument of the Union about the vagueness of the chargeshee): 
.tl>us, has no strength. , - 

8 . While dealing wilh the manner in which the enquiry proceedings were 
conducted, Shri Rudra with a forceful manner attempted to argue that the enquiry 
does net prove the cause lead:ng to the resultant alleged abuse and assauU. As 
the charge.sheet v.'as, not foi the neglect in duty on the part of the workman 
(checking unauthorised robbing of coal etc-), there does not appear to be any 
reason, why this aspect should have been focussed during the enquiry. It is 
interesting to note that during the cross-examination of the P.W I, Shri Budhan 
Mahato, the delinquent bas posed questions that while he was the only Guard at 
the Depot how was it possible for him to see the whole depot area. This itself 
proves that the debEc uent does' not deny the happening of the robbing of the coal 
by unauthorised rersons. Anyway, there is nothing to prove that the piinciples 
of natural justice were not followed by the Enquiry Officer, wtoile holding the 
enquiry on Ifith May, 1970. The rules of natural justice require that a party 
should have the oppoiturnty of adducing of relevant evidence on which he relies, 
that the evidence of the opponent should be taken in Ids presence, and that he 
should be given the opportunity of cross-examining the witnesses examined by 
that party, and that no materials should be relied on against him without his 
being given an opportunity of explaining thern. i.n the instant case a minute 
scrutiny of the enquiry papers revealed that all these requirements have been 
followed properly. X do not find any reason in not relying on the contentions of 
the employer.? in regard to the manner in which the enquiry was conducted. 

9. Let me now examine the third and the final argument putforth by Shri 
Rudra on behalf of the workman in regard to the proving of the charge iWelled 
against the workman. The charge is mainly for abasing and assat Tin'r Shri 
Budhan Mahato. Durirg the entire enquiry proceedings, the workman did not 
deny his abusing and assaulting Shri Budhan Mahato. During the enquiry, Shri 
Budhan Mahato has deposed at length how he was abused and assaulted by the 
workman. Immediately after the incident. Shri Mahato went to his quarters and 
as the assault made by stick on him was giving lot of pam, he had to go to the 
Colliery Medical Officer for treatment. Dr. M. S. Saha, who was P.W, 5 has ^dated 
during the enquiry ihat Shri Budhan Mahato was treated at the Colliery Dispen- 
sary On loth November, 1969 and the outdoor patient register records the entry 
“pain and swelling — left thigh” against the entry of Shri Budhan Mahato. Shri 
Ashoke Chakraborty, P.W. 3 had deposed during the enquiry that he was a witne.ss 
to the incident of assault on Shri Mahato by Shri Shyamdeo Singh. He actually 
intervened in the incident and separated the two. About the abuse, it has been 
stated during the enquiry by Shri Mahato that the workman had hurled abuses 
at him viz Badmash etc. Thus, there is enough evidence proving the charge "of 
abuse and assault by the workman. 

10. Having therefore carefully considered the facts of the case and the evidences 
adduced during the enquiry as revealed from the enquiry papers, and other mate- 
rials placed before me by the parties and further having considered the pleadings 
during hearing of the case and in view of the aforesaid facts, I hold that the 
action of the management in dismissing Shri Shyamdeo Singh. Der-ot Gfuard from 
letb July, 1970 was justified. In view of this the question of any relief to the 
workman does not arise. This isi my AWARD.

excellent cooperation given to me during the course of arbilratioii hearing

emoluments of tne workmen as payable on 25th Auguit, 1967 in accorda ce with

outstanding duos o>i account of Wages, Bonus (Quarterly), Profit Sharing Bonus. 
Leave pay and one way Railway Fare and such other dues as the Workers may 
be entitled to get. 


5. That the Management also agrees to pay a lump sum of Rs. 400 (Rupees 
Four Hundred only) in addition to the retrenchment i^onipensation and the outstand- 
ing due mentioned in clauses 1 & 3 above, to each workman. 

6. That the above payments shall be made on or before 15th May, 1971 and in 
case of failure on the part of the Management to make the payments by 15th May, 
1971, the Management shall be liable to pay to each yrorkman additional Rs. 10/- 
for each week cf such delay. If any workman fails to turn up on the appointed 
date of payment to be notified by the Management the dues of such workman shall 
be sent by M.O. to his address as may be furnished by the workman. 

7. That the workmen shall have no claim of reinstatement or any other claim 
on the management after they are paid in terms of this settlement. 


8. That with legard to Shri R. L. Boxi, it is agreed that the management shall 
bring him back on the roll of the Colliery and shall give him all the benefits he 
has been getting (Quarterly Bonus. P. S. Bonus, Leave Wages etc.) and pay him a 
lump sum of Rs. 1000 for compensating the losses he suffered as a result of his

AGREEMENT 


Short recital of the case 

Shri Kaplldeo Mahto, working as pump khalasi was stopped from work with 
effect from 8ih Juno, 1970. He remained out of employment till 24th June, 1970, 
An Indu.strlal dispute for non employment of the workman and wages for the period 
of idleness was raised by the union which ended in failure In conciliation proceed- 
ing before the A. L. C. (C) Dhanbad-I. The Government of India In the Ministry 
of Labour and Employment referred this dispute for adjudication to the Central 
Government Industrial Tribunal No. 2 at Dhanbad vide notification No. 2/118/70- 
LHII dated 6th October, 1.970, 

After 'reference of this dispute to the Tribunal the parties set and resalved this 
difference among themselves and have settled the differences on the following 
terms. 


Terms of Agreement 

1 The management agrees to pay halt wages to the workmen Kaplldeo- 
Mahto "for the period 8th June, 1970, to 24th June, 1970, in presence 
of the representatives of the Panchayat. 

2, That the continuity of service of Sri Kaplldeo Mahto shall not be affected 
any way due to break In his service due to stoppage of work.

After the reference of this dispute for ad.iudlcatlon the management represen- 
tative and the Union representative sat and dlscu.ssed to resolve the dispute in the 
interest of mutual good relation and industrial peace. As a result of this mutual 
discussions parties have arrived at a settlement. 

It is requested that this tribunal be pleased to give award in terms of this 
settlement. 


Terms of Settlement 

1. That the management have paid full retrenchment compensation to all 
workmen as laid down in Section of the I.D. Act, except Serial Nos. 12, 20, 
21, 34, 40, 41, 58 and 64 i.e. Sri J. C. Dasandhu, Sri Awadhesh Pandey, Sri Shyam 
Sunder Singh, Sambhu Mallik, Gopi Hazzam, Hemraj Barhi, Mahatam Saw and 
Nefar Rabidas.

question of his reinstatement does not arise. 

3 . That the management have re-employed serials Nos. 1, 2. 3. 4 and 5 i.e. Sri 
B. B. Singh, Sri M. L. Nunia, Sri S. K. Singh, Sri B. N. Mahata, and Sri B. K. 
sural and they have also been paid full compensation as laid down under Section 
26F of the I. D. Act. 

4. That the management though have paid full retrenchment compensation as 
laid down under Section, 25F of the Act, undertake to re-employ them in the 
group of Collieries owned by this management excepting Serial Nos. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 as 
they haf\re already been employed and Serial No. 83 who has died. 

5. That the management and the Union undertake to inform the workmen to 
join their duties by the 15th dav from the receipt of the award. 

6. That, if any workmen fails to report for duty by the 15th day from the 
receipt of the award even after intimation shall forflet his claim for re-employment. 

7. That serial No. 12, 20, 21, 34, 40, 41. 58 & 64 namgly Sri J. C, Dasandhi, Sri 
Awadhesh Pandey. Sri Shyam Sundar Singh, Sri Sambhu Mallik, Sri Gopi Hazzam 
Sri Hem Raj Barhi, Sli Mahatam Saw, and Sri Nefar Rabidas shall be reinstated 
and shall be paid compen.salion as has been paid to other workmen in the ll.st of 
reference. 

8. That continuity of service shall be given to all the workmen who resume their 
duty within the prescribed time limit. 

This agreement as stated above settle.s the claims of all workmen mentioned 
in the list of the reference.

AWARD 

The Central Government having received a written aw'eement in pursuance 
of the provisions of sub-section (1) of Section lOA of the Industrial Disputes 
Act, 1947 (14 of 1947) between the management of Bankola Colliery of Mis. The 
Burrakur Coal Co. Ltd,, P.O. Ukhra, Dist. Burdwan (hereinafter referred to as 
the management) and their workman represented by the Colliery Mazdoor Sabha 
(.AITUC), G.T. Road, AsanaoT (hereinafter referred to as the union) referring 
the industrial dispute between them, the specific matters in dispute being as 
detailed below to my arbitration, and the Central Government being of the opi- 
nion that the Industrial dispute referred to above existed between the manage- 
ment and the union, ordered publication of the said arbitration agreement in the 
(Gazette of India, Part II, Section 3, sub-section (ii) under its order No. 8| 11 8170- 
LR.II, dated the 29th August, 1970: 

“Specific matters in dispute: 

What should be the nucleus (permanent) strength of the Wagon Loaders 
of Bankola Colliery of M|s. Burrakur Coal Co. Ltd., P.O. Ukhra, 
Dist. Burdwan.” 

2. The General Secretary of the Colliery Mazdoor Sabha (AITUC), Asansol 
was requested under my letter No. E. 1|10(2)|70, dated 22nd July, 1970 to submit 
written statement on behalf of the workmen endorsing a copy thereof to the 
management under intimation to me and simultaneously the Manager, Bankola 
Colliery was requested to submit written statement on behalf of the manage- 
ment and also rejoinder, if any, on the written statement of the workmen under 
my letter No. E. 1|10(2)|70, dated 22nd July, 1970, endorsing a copy thereof to 
the union. The union had submitted written statement on 13th August, 1970, 
which was received by me on 13th August, 1970. The management had submitted 
written statement on 27th August, 1970 which was received by me on 27th 
August, 1970. In forwarding a copy of the written statement, dated 27th August. 
1970 of the management to the union under my letter No. E. 1110(2)170, dated 
29th August, 1970, the union was requested to submit rejoinder on the written 
statement of the management so as to reach me latest by ?th September, 1970. 
The union submitted rejoinder on 23rd November, 1070 which was received by 
me on 26th November, 1970. Thereafter the matter came up for' hearing on 
5th December, 1970. On 5th December. 1970 Shri J, Sharan, Personnel Officer 
0 )' the management was present on behalf of the management and Shri Sunil 
Ssn, Organising Secretary, Colliery Mazdoor Sabha was present on behalf of 
the workmen. ITie representative of the union filed a petition requesting there- 
in to call upon the management to produce certain documents which the repre- 
sentative of the management assured to produce. After preliminary discussions 
the hearing was adjourned with the consent of the representatives of both 
parties. I fixed up hearing In the matter on 28th December, 1970 but In the mean- 
time the representative of the management filed petition dated 21st December 



Sec. ^^iin THE GAZEITE OF INDIA: APRIL 24, 1971/VAlSAKHA 4, 1893 2097 


.1970 and requested to adjourn the hearing, With the consent of the 
pariie.i the hearing was adjourned to ibe held in my ollice on 5th January, 1971, 
On 5th January, 1971 Shri M. K. Mulcherjee, Advocate, A.sansol and Shri J. Sha- 
ran, .Hersonnel Officer were present on behalf of the management. Shri Sunil 
Sen, Orgam.sing Secretai'y, Colliery Mazdoor Sabha, Asansol was present on be- 
bali of the workmen. On that date the management submitted a rejoinder, dated 
5th January, 1971 to the writteh statement of the union and a copy of the same 
was handed over to the representative of the union. After preliminary hearing 
I called upon the representative of the Tnanagement to submit monthly state- 
ment showing the number of ordinary wagons and box wagons indicating their 
capaciiy, .supplied on each day of the month, showing the number of such wagons 
loaded mechanically as well as manually each day separately for the 
ntunths of January, 1970 to Decemiber, 1970. After this, with the con- 
sent of the parties the hearing was adjourned to be held in my office on 14th 
January, 1971. On 14th January, 1971 Shri J. Sharan, Personnel Officer was pre- 
sent on behalf of the management and Shri Sunil Sen, Organising Secretary was 
present on behalf of the workmen. On behalf of the management 35 papers] 
documents were filed which with the consent of the representative of the work- 
men were marked Exls. M.l(a) to M.l(d), M.2(a) to M.2(h), Ext. 3, Ext. 4(al to 
4(hl, E^t.',=,(a) to 5(c), Ext. 6(a) to 6(c), Ext. 7(a) to E.xt. ltd) and Ext. 8(a) to 
Ext. 8(a). The representative of the workmen, however, did, not file any docu- 
mentary evidence. On behalf of the managciliShT Shri S. K, Singh, Superinten- 
dent (MW.l) was produced as witness. He was examined and cross-examined. 
On behalf of workmen one witness namely Shri Bancha Hondol (WW.l) was 
produced. He was examined and cross-examined. In the meantime the parties 
entered into an agreement agreeing therein that I might give my award in the 
instant industrial dispute latest by 31.st March, 1971. Finally, I heard the argu- 
ment.s of both the parties on 22nd January, 1971. 

3. The case of the union as made out in their written .statement is in brief 
that large number of wagon loaders had been engaged by the management for 
long time and they were not being paid wages directly and that their attendances 
were not marked properly and they were not paid bonus and other benefits; that 
the management had resorted to unfair labour practices in as much as they 
had kept them as temporary and changed their names from time to time to 
■deprive them of their legitimate legal dues etc.; that the wagon loaders had been 
agitating since long for their proper marking of attendances, direct payment and 
also lor increase in the number of permanent workmen; that as a result of pro- 
longed agitation, the management agreed to make direct payment since January, 
1970; that the average monthly despatch figure of coal loaded into wagons by 
hand loading was to the tune of 40,000 tonnes per month; that in view of the 
same the number of permanent wagon loaders was very poor but in.spite of the 
agitation by the wagon loader.s tho management remained adamant and did not 
increase the number of permanent wagon_ loaders and on the contrary the manage- 
ment adopted the policy of improper di.stribution of wagon.s to the temporary 
wagon loader.s with a view to harassing them; that , the management was bound 
to increase the number of permanent wagon loaders as per the direction of the 
law on the ba.si.s of average monthly despatch figure with retrospective effect. 
Finally the union demanded that all the 125 wagon loaders enlisted in its written 
statement be made permanent with retrospective effect, In their rejoinder to 
the written statement submitted by the management, the union submitted that 
the monthly despatch of coal through wagon loading was to the tune of 40,000 
tonnes but denied that the joy loader and conveyor belt could load 28,000 tonnes 
per month and also denied that hand loading on an average in a month was to 
tho tune of 12,000 tonnes as contended by the management. It was further assert- 
ed by the union that the average hand loading was 32,000 tonnes per month and 
accordingly the nucleus permanent strength of wagon loaders would be 255 against 
which the nucleus permanent strength of wagon loaders in the colliery was only 
110 and it was demanded that all the wagon loaders enlisted in its written state- 
ment ibe directed to be declared as permanent with retrospective effect. 

4, The case of the management as made out in Its written statement In brief 
is that the average despatch of coal through wagon loading Is to the tune of 
40,000 tonnes per month; that out of these total despatch, the conveyor belt and 
joy loader haye the capacity to load to the tune of 28,000 tonnes per month cal- 
culated on the basis of 26 working days in a month or In other words 20 box 
wagons per day equivalent to 11,00 tonnes per day; that the average loading of 
coal by wagon loaders through manual loading comes to the tune of about 12,000 
tonnes per month; that on an average a wagon loader Is supposed to load 4.5 



2098 the gazette of INDIA; APRIL 24, 1971/VAISAKHA 4, 1893 [Part II- 


ton per day on the basis of the recommendations of the Central Wage Board for 
the Coal Mining Industry; that on this basis the total requirement of wagon 
loaders comes to 102 wagon loaders but as against this the management has per- 
manent nucleus strength of 110, wagon loaders; that the allegations of the union 
as incorporated in its written statement, wart from being artificial are irrelevant 
in respect of the issue under reference. Therefore, the management prayed that 
the strength of the permanent wagon loaders was more than adequate. The 
management in its rejoinder to the written statement submitted tav the Colliery 
Mazdoor Sabha submitted further that the management had completely abolished 
the contract labour system in respect of coal loading and unloading and there- 
fore determination and/or review of nucleus strength of the wagon loaders in 
accordance with the agreement adopted toy the Dave Court of Enquiry was 
neither legally permissible nor could it be the subject matter for any arbitration. 
Moreover, without prejudice to the above contention the management contenefed. 
that during the proceeding three months viz., October, 1970, November, 1970 
and Decenaber, 1970 the average monthly despatch of coal came to 36,210 metric 
tonnes and keeping in view the cipacHy of the conveyor belt and joy loader the 
number of wagon loaders required as nucleus strength came to about 60 cal- 
culated on the basis of work load of T.5 tonnes. The capacity of wagon loaders- 
of the management was to load much more than 4.5 tonnes per day ana, therefore, 
after taking the same into consideration, the nucleus strength would be even 
les.s than 66. The management denied the contention of the union that the- aver- 
age loading by hand woked out to 32,000 tonnes per month, in order to avoid 
laying off of permanent wagon loaders when supply position of wagons was poor 
or wlien there was break down of machineries, the machine loading was either 
suspended or reduced to provide employment to alL the permanent wagon loaders. 

6. During the course of healing it was admitted by both parties that the 
system of engaging wagon loaders through contractors was abolished in Bankolai 
Colliery of the management since January, 1970. It was, therefore, pressed very 
much by Shri M, K. Mukherjee, advocate, learned representative of the manage- 
ment that in accordance with the report of the Central Government Court of 
Enquiry (Coal Mining Industry), Calcutta, in the industrial dispute between the 
employers in relation to coal mining industry and their workmen over the aboli- 
tion of the contract labour system m coal mining industry the management was 
required to maintain a nucleus strength of wagon loaders only when the work of 
coal loading and unloading was done through the contractors. As the manage- 
ment in the instant case had abolished the contract labour system with regard 
to the loading and unloading of coal since January, 1979 and continued to engage 
all wagon loaders directly, there wa.s absolutely no Point in my determining the 
strength of nucleus of wagon loaders. I reproduce below para 27 of the report 
of the said court of enquiry which reads as under:— 

“27. The fourth category is coal loading and unloading. The agreement on 
this category contains a proviso that the principal employer shall 
engage a nucleus of wagon and truck loaders to whom regular work 
can be guaranteed the number of such nucleus to be reviewed 
quarterly. I am told that the position of wagon supply is not regular 
and sometimes extra work of loading and unloading may be required 
to be done, and that such w.ork would be of temporary nature 
and hence it is said that it may be allowed to be done through con- 
tractors. This is, however, likely to load to mal-practices. For ins- 
tance an employer may engage a nucleus of loaders at much less 
than his normal and usual requirements and thus evade doing the 
work departmentally. I am, however, accepting the agreement only 
because it Is agreed that it would be reviewed later on.” 

I also reproduce below relevant extracts of the agreement dated the 30th 
October, 1961 between the rcprcsentative.s of the coal industry and their work- 
men which formed part of the said report: — 

“2. It is further agreed that all processes directly connected with the rais- 
ing and despatch of coal and manufacture and despatch of coke shall 
he the direct responsibility of the principal employer and all workers 
engaged therein shall he the employees of the principal employer 
except in the following categories: — 

* * • * * * • 

(iv) Coal loading and unloading provided that the Principal Employer 
shall engage a nucleus of wagon loaders to whom regular work can 
be guranteed; the number of such nucleus to he reviewed quarterly,”" 



Sec. 3(ii)] THE GAZETTE OF INDIA: APRIL 24, 1971/VALSAKHA 4, 1893 3O99 


There is force in the argument of Shri Mukherjee in this regard, but at the same 
time the specific matters in dispute before me which is also reproduced below 
cannot be lost sight of: — 

“Specific matters in dispute: 

What should be the nucleus (permanent) strength of the wagon loaders of 

Bankola Colliery of M/s. Burrakur Coal Co. Ltd., P.O. Ukhra, Distt. 

Burdwan.” 

In the terms of reference there is no mention of the report of the court of enquiry 
referred to above, on the contrary the njeaning of the word, ‘nucleus’ which the 
parties had in their minds while referring the dispute to my arbitration has been 
precisely made clear by inserting the word, ‘permanent’ in bracket after the word, 
‘nucleus’. The terms of reference in the arbitration agreement in question clearly 
goes to show that the parties want me to determine the permanent strength of 
the wagon loaders in Bankola Colliery of the management or in other words I am 
required to determine the strength of the permanent wagon loaders in the colliery. 

6. With regard to the basis on which the strength of the permanent wagon 
loaders should be determined, divergent views have been placed before me by 
the learned representatives of both partis. Shri Sunil Sen learned representative 
of the workmen argued before me that the strength of the permanent wagon 
loaders should be determined on the basis of average quantity of coal actually 
loaded into wagons at the colliery through hand loading. He further stated that 
Exts. M.I(a) to M.I(d) which are the statements submitted by the managements 
showing the number of ordinary wagons loaded manually and through machines 
and the number of box wagon-s loaded manually and through machines each day 
for the months of January, 1970 to December, 1970 show that 32,567 tonnes of 
coal were loaded manually on an average in a month or in other words about 20 
box wagons were required to be loaded manually on an average each day. IJhus 
according to him the strength of permanent wagon loaders came to ' 243. ' As 
against this the learned representative of the management hrgued that the figures 
of quantity of coal actually loaded into wagons manually during the period 
January, 1P,70 to December, 1970 should not be taken as criteria fpr deterpiining 
the strength of the permanent wagon loaders in as much gs the wagon loaders 
forcibly did not allow the management to utilise the full capacity of two machines 
in operation in loa;ding coal into wagons and on the contrary the wagon loaders 
used to forcibly loaoT the coal into wagons manually. Moreover, whenever 
number of wagons allotted to the colliery was inadequate to the management 
either stopped mechanical loading altogether or reduced the number of wagons to 
be loaded mechanically and diverted wagons for manual loading in order to pro- 
vide work to the wagon loaders with a view to avoiding labour unrest. Besides, the 
wagons were diverted for manual loading some times because of breakdown 
in machines and some times on account of defects in the brakes of the wagons 
rendering them unsuitable for mechanical loading. In this connection the manage- 
ment placed reliance on the warning letters issued to various gang heads of 
wagon loaders vide Exl.s, M. (2a) to M.2(h). M.4(a't to M.4(h) as well a.s on the 
statement of Shri S, K. Singh, Superintendent (MW.I). ’The exhibits referred 
to above do Indicate that the wagon loaders had forcibly loaded the wagons whioh 
were meant for mechanical loading,. I also reproduce the relevant extracts of 
the statement of Shri S. K. Singh (MW.I): — 

At Bankola Colliery loading of coal Into wagons is being done mechanically 
as well as munually. There are two machines which are in use for the purpose 
of loading coal ipto wagons. In addition to these two machines there is one more 
machine but it is lying idle for the pre.sent but it can be used for that purpose. 
One of the machines which is in use has the capacity of loading of over 550 
tonnes per day or in other words ten box wagons. Rest of the two machines has 
the capacity of loading more than ten box wagons per day each. In normal 
circumstances i.e, when there is no breakdown and the supply of wagon is ade- 
quate twenty box wagons have been loaded in one day. As per the record we 
had loaded 19-1/4 box wagons on 18tb July, 1970, 20-2/3 box wagons on 23rd July, 
1970. 19-1/2 box wagons on 30th July, 1970. The full capacity of the machines for 
loading wagons cannot be achieved on all the days because of (i) irregular sup- 
ply Of wagons, (ii) breakdown in the machineries, Hii) forcible loading of wagons 
by casual wagon loaders and (iv) defective brakes in the box wagons. On 
some days when the number of wagons supplied is less, we provide the wagons 
to the permanent wagon loaders by stopping mechanical loading to avoid laying 
off of permanent wagon loaders. If we put into commission the third machine 
which is at present idle we will have’ to reduce the number of permanent wagon 
loaders. It will not be possible to utilise such of the wagons which have defective 



2100 the gazette of INDIA: APRIL 24, 1971/VAISAKHA 4, 1893 [Part II— 


brakes for mechanical loading because for the mechanical loading the movement 
of the wagon is to be precisely controlled lest they move away from the mecha- 
nical loading point. That is why those defective wagons are utilised only for 
manual loading.
